### Tool result

_source line 12 · 2026-05-21T21:52:54.945Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TJ6o8PXTb898XuTdw4fYWp`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	Recursive generation alters \emph{where} the token-level surprise distribution sits but not \emph{how} it is shaped, a dichotomy with a mechanistic basis.
5	Under nucleus sampling, top-$p$ truncation preserves the functional form of the conditional distribution at each step; what changes is the \emph{input}: as the model conditions on increasingly self-typical outputs, expected NLL compresses monotonically.
6	This yields two testable predictions: (1)~increasing sampling randomness should weaken the location drift, and (2)~semantic-preservation constraints should attenuate it.
7	Both are confirmed: at $t{=}1.0$, location signal collapses ($\rho$: $-0.60 \to -0.002$; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); under elaboration prompts, drift weakens (F1\,=\,0.730 vs.\ 0.781; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
8	Causal analyses provide supporting evidence: drifting features contribute $5.5\times$ more to classification, with 6/7 exhibiting significant Granger causality over the five-point chain, though the short series length (5 depths) limits formal causal inference (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-causality}).
9	These results are established under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$); shape features remain stable even at $\tau{=}1.0$ ($d < 0.15$; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).
10	Greedy decoding ($\tau{=}0$) confirms shape stability across all eight generators (mean $|d|{=}0.05$), establishing DDR as an autoregressive property independent of sampling truncation; top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$) compresses drifting effect sizes without introducing new shape instability, with boundary violations in Pythia/OLMo traceable to model-intrinsic baselines (Appendix~\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).
11	
12	The classifier is a proof-of-concept for corpus-level auditing, not per-document filtering: gen-1 per-class F1\,=\,0.63 is insufficient for individual flagging but supports batch-level contamination estimation (${\pm}3$--$4$\% at $n{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\ref{app:error-analysis}).
13	The three-class resolution enables depth-stratified corpus curation (retaining gen-1 content while flagging recursively degraded material), a distinction invisible to binary classifiers.
14	Depth-aware filtering can serve as a prophylactic against model collapse \cite{drayson_detection_prevents_collapse}, though depth-stratified curation does not yet improve downstream training (TOST equivalence at $\varepsilon{=}{\pm}2\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:downstream}); the primary value lies in provenance, not training improvement.
15	
16	DDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage by updating weights, whereas inference-chain drift at fixed parameters shifts only location, leaving shape stable.
17	Prior work establishes \emph{that} convergence occurs \cite{markovian_chains,curious_decline} without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved; DDR supplies this decomposition, isolating which distributional properties carry cross-generator depth signal, enabling detection without per-generator calibration.
18	Three depth classes define the practical resolution ceiling (5-class F1\,$\approx$\,0.50; Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}); QLoRA recovers more signal (F1\,=\,0.905, 6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), reflecting token-order patterns that aggregate statistics miss.
19	Instruction-tuned models compress DDR structure (3-class F1 degrades 3.7--19.9\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), yet QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91, confirming token-level depth signal beyond summary-statistic decomposition.
20	Cross-domain evaluation (News, Academic, Code; $t{=}1.0$) yields binary F1\,=\,0.74--0.75 but degraded 3-class F1 (0.62--0.65; Appendix~\ref{app:domain}), with temperature and domain effects confounded.
21	Our generation chains use open-ended continuation prompts; real-world recursive pipelines (instruction-following chains, multi-turn dialogues, mixed-model editing) may attenuate, restructure, or introduce confounds not present in controlled chains.
22	
```

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Depth Classification Performance}
5	\label{sec:results-classification}
6	
7	The 7-feature ensemble achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.762 (95\% CI [0.758, 0.766]; $N$\,=\,37{,}058) across eight generators from five families (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}), with cross-generator F1 spread of 9.1\,pp capturing generator selection uncertainty.
8	Because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}), gen-0 classification is near-perfect (per-class F1\,$\approx$\,0.94), inflating the 3-class metric.
9	We therefore adopt \textbf{leak-free binary F1} (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+)\,=\,0.694 (CI [0.688, 0.700]) as the primary metric throughout: it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing.
10	Per-generator binary F1 ranges from 0.633 (Mistral-7B) to 0.724 (Pythia-2.8B; SD\,=\,0.028), with the primary difficulty concentrated at the gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ boundary (Appendix~\ref{app:confusion}).
11	CIs reflect bootstrap sampling variability; the cross-generator range (9.1\,pp) captures the distinct and larger uncertainty of generator selection.
12	
13	\paragraph{Cross-model generalization.}
14	Cross-model chains (randomly selected generator per step) yield binary F1\,=\,0.830 (95\% CI [.818, .841]) under 5-generator LOGO.\footnote{5-generator subset (Pythia-2.8B/1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B/1.7B); generator-level LOGO with bootstrap CIs ($n$\,=\,2{,}000). Not directly comparable to 8-generator LOGO due to differing subsets.}
15	Single-generator transfer (Appendix~\ref{app:transfer}) establishes that the signal is not a mixed-chain artifact: classifiers trained on one generator transfer to all others with mean binary F1\,=\,0.810; LOFO yields 0.686.
16	A scale effect emerges: Pythia achieves the highest F1 (0.718--0.724) while larger models degrade (Mistral-7B: 0.633), partly reflecting same-family affinity with the Pythia-410M reference (cross-reference spread $\leq$0.013; \S\ref{sec:results-differential}); cross-domain signal persists with variation (Appendix~\ref{app:domain}).
17	An end-to-end QLoRA classifier achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 under 6-generator LOGO ($+$11.6\,pp vs.\ the same-subset XGBoost baseline of 0.789; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming that richer token-level representations capture additional depth signal; on instruct data, QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91 within-model (Appendix~\ref{app:instruct}), establishing the empirical decomposition, not the specific classifier, as DDR's primary contribution.
18	
19	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
20	Length-matched subsampling yields binary F1\,=\,0.724 (vs.\ 0.770 unmatched); a random-subsample control verifies the drop reflects deconfounding, not sample-size reduction (Appendix~\ref{app:length}).
21	
22	\paragraph{Seed-sharing control.}
23	Seed-level demeaning (F1\,=\,0.701) and an independent-seed experiment with disjoint seed pools (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}) both verify that the classifier captures generative degradation, not seed-specific content.
24

26	\centering
27	\footnotesize
28	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
29	\begin{tabular}{llcccc}
30	\toprule
31	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{3-cl F1}$^{\dagger}$ \\
32	\midrule
33	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 2.8B & \textbf{0.724} & [.707, .742] & 0.798 \\
34	Pythia-1B       & Pythia  & 1.0B & 0.718 & [.700, .735] & \textbf{0.806} \\
35	Qwen3-1.7B     & Qwen    & 1.7B & 0.709 & [.693, .726] & 0.772 \\

37	OLMo-2-1B      & OLMo    & 1.5B & 0.695 & [.679, .711] & 0.781 \\

Binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is the primary metric; it excludes gen-0 entirely and is unaffected by seed sharing. Cross-generator binary F1: SD\,=\,0.028, IQR\,=\,[0.692, 0.714]. $^{\dagger}$3-class F1 carries upward bias because gen-0 seeds are shared across generators (\S\ref{sec:generation}); independent-seed controls confirm the depth signal (F1\,=\,0.680; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-control}).}
47	\label{tab:logo-results}

49	
50	\subsection{Differential Depth Response}
51	\label{sec:results-differential}
52	
53	\begin{figure*}[t]
54	\centering
55	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_ddr_trajectory.pdf}
56	\caption{Feature trajectories across generation depth. \textbf{Top row (drifting)}: entropy mean and hapax ratio decline monotonically across all eight generators ($|\rho| > 0.59$). \textbf{Bottom row (stationary)}: entropy max and tail decay remain flat ($|\rho| < 0.07$). Colored lines: individual generators; diamonds: cross-generator mean (blue for drifting, amber for stationary). This dichotomy defines the differential depth response (DDR).}
57	\label{fig:ddr-trajectory}
58	\end{figure*}
59	
60	Recursive generation does not affect all distributional properties uniformly.
61	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trajectory} exposes a consistent dichotomy across all eight generators: central-tendency features (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio) decline monotonically with depth, while shape features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain flat.
62	This asymmetry constitutes the \emph{differential depth response} (DDR).
63	The primary partition is binary: $k{=}2$ clustering in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space yields silhouette\,=\,0.660 (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}), cleanly separating three \emph{drifting} features ($|\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) from four \emph{non-drifting} ones ($d \leq 0.55$); higher $k$ degrades cluster quality ($k{=}3$: silhouette\,=\,0.532).
64	A domain-informed refinement resolves entropy std as a sole \emph{intermediate} member ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$), distinct from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$; CV shift $<$3\% vs.\ $>$15\% for drifting features).
65	Multiple effect-size measures corroborate this partition, establishing that the asymmetry reflects distributional structure rather than feature-selection artifact (Quantification below).
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t!]
68	\centering
69	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_differential_response.pdf}
70	\caption{Differential depth response (DDR). Diamonds: cross-generator means; dots: per-generator values. Drifting features (blue; $|\rho| > 0.60$, $d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) show strong monotonic depth sensitivity; non-drifting features remain stable. Entropy std (wine; $d{=}0.55$) is an intermediate member; near-stationary features (amber; $d \leq 0.10$) show negligible drift. Entropy min ($|\rho|{=}0.40$ pooled; gen-0/gen-1 sensitive) and tail decay (TOST 4/8) are boundary features within this group.}
71	\label{fig:feature-drift}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	Pooled class-balanced Spearman correlations with depth corroborate the binary split (Figure~\ref{fig:feature-drift}):
75	drifting features show substantial monotonic trends (hapax ratio $\rho = -0.64$, TTR $\rho = -0.63$, entropy mean $\rho = -0.60$),
76	while non-drifting features remain near-zero (tail decay $|\rho| = 0.04$; per-depth distributions in Appendix~\ref{app:feature-distributions}).
77	The six-fold magnitude gap between drifting ($|\rho| > 0.60$) and non-drifting features ($|\rho| \leq 0.16$) defines the DDR partition.
78	Within the non-drifting group, entropy std ($|\rho| = 0.16$, $d{=}0.55$) shows a substantial level shift despite low monotonicity, distinguishing it from the three near-stationary features ($d < 0.10$); this refinement is stable across $d$ thresholds in $[0.56, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
79	
80	\paragraph{Quantification.}
81	Parametric and non-parametric effect-size measures converge on the split.
82	Cohen's $d$ \cite{cohen1988statistical} shows a ${\geq}7\times$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and near-stationary features ($d \leq 0.08$); KS statistics, Glass's $\Delta$, Cliff's $\delta$, and CV shift all corroborate (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
83	Four features fall cleanly into drifting or non-drifting groups.
84	Entropy min and tail decay are \emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1 ($|\rho|{=}0.40$); tail decay is \emph{weakly stationary}, passing TOST for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).
85	Entropy std ($d{=}0.55$, $|\rho|{=}0.16$) is the sole intermediate member.
86	\paragraph{Validation.}
87	Per-generator split analysis provides the strongest validation: each of the eight hold-out generators independently recovers the identical drifting/non-drifting partition (per-generator silhouette range 0.505--0.760; Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
88	$k{=}2$ clustering in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space corroborates this separation (silhouette\,=\,0.660), invariant across threshold choices $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$.
89	Per-generator TOST equivalence testing ($\varepsilon{=}|d|{<}0.20$, following Cohen's~\citeyearpar{cohen1988statistical} conventional threshold; gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8 pass) and entropy max (6/8); tail decay passes for only 4/8, with $|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$ spanning the boundary.
90	A permutation test ($n{=}10{,}000$) establishes the three-vs-four split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$; fixed-label silhouette\,=\,0.660 vs.\ null mean\,=\,0.46); exhaustive enumeration of all $\binom{7}{3}{=}35$ possible splits ranks this partition first (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-validation}).
91	Reclassifying either boundary feature as intermediate preserves the core drifting/non-drifting split and leaves classification unchanged.
92	
93	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
94	The full 7-feature set (F1\,=\,0.773, 10-fold stratified CV) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \approx 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablation-table}).
95	PCA confirms the three drifting features share a single latent dimension (92.3\% variance; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}); replacing them with PC1 costs only 1.1\,pp, reinforcing that DDR's contribution is identifying \emph{which} distributional properties drift, not classification per se.
96	TTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are both retained for marginal LOFO gains despite feature masking \cite{strobl_conditional_2008} (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}).
97	
98	\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}
99	Alternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable LOGO F1 (spread 0.013; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-ref}), bounding the same-family affinity effect; depth features are robust to reference model choice, though residual same-family proximity may inflate Pythia-family F1 by up to 1.3\,pp.
100	
101	\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}
102	All four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-9.3$ to $-26.0$\,pp binary F1); low-temperature improvement is family-dependent ($+7$--$9$\,pp for Pythia/OLMo at $t{=}0.5$, negligible for Qwen; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}).
103	Continuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\,=\,0.781), with cross-prompt-type transfer confirming shared signal (LOPTO F1\,=\,0.753; Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}); the DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures despite absolute effect-size compression (Appendix~\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).
104	
105	
106	\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}
107	\label{sec:results-baselines}
108	
109	Existing detection methods target binary real-vs-synthetic classification; we test whether their continuous scores incidentally encode recursive depth by repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\ref{tab:baselines}).
110	
111	\begin{table}[t]
112	\centering
113	\footnotesize
114	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
115	\toprule
116	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{Feat.} & \textbf{3-cl F1} & \textbf{Bin F1} & \textbf{ms/text} \\
117	\midrule
118	4-det.\ + surface     & 6 & \textbf{0.795} & \textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\
119	4-detector ens.\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\
120	7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \textbf{9.7} \\
121	\midrule
122	Avg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\
123	FastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\
124	LD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\
125	Binoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\
126	\bottomrule
127	\end{tabular}
128	\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled evaluation; pooled F1 is slightly higher than per-generator macro F1 in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} because larger test folds from high-performing generators contribute proportionally more). Identical XGBoost configuration. 7-feature: one 410M model (905\,MB); 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B params (2{,}868\,MB). Individual detection scores (N/A) share the reference-model forward pass; per-method timing is not separately profiled.}
129	\label{tab:baselines}
130	\end{table}
131	
132	\begin{figure}[t]
133	\centering
134	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}
135	\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\ inference cost in ms/text (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M reference model) matches multi-detector ensembles (two models, 1.4B total) at 2.4$\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth. Inference costs measured on a single NVIDIA RTX 5090.}
136	\label{fig:baseline-ci}
137	\end{figure}
138	
139	The 7-feature ensemble surpasses all individual baselines in 3-class F1 ($\Delta$\,=\,6.7--36.3\,pp; Figure~\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) and the pure 4-detector ensemble in binary F1 (0.705 vs.\ 0.700).
140	Adding DDR's surface features to the four detectors yields the highest binary F1 (0.708), marginally above the 7-feature set at 2.4$\times$ the inference cost.
141	This result underscores that DDR's primary contribution is not classification performance (multi-detector ensembles match or marginally exceed it) but the decomposition of depth signal into location-sensitive and shape-stable components, a structural distinction absent from both binary detection methods and model collapse analyses (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
142

this refinement is stable across $d$ thresholds in $[0.56, 0.75]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Quantification.}\nParametric and non-parametric effect-size measures converge on the split.\nCohen's $d$ \\cite{cohen1988statistical} shows a ${\\geq}7\\times$ gap between drifting ($d{=}0.76$--$1.17$) and near-stationary features ($d \\leq 0.08$); KS statistics, Glass's $\\Delta$, Cliff's $\\delta$, and CV shift all corroborate (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nFour features fall cleanly into drifting or non-drifting groups.\nEntropy min and tail decay are \\emph{boundary features}: entropy min is near-stationary for gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+ ($d{=}0.016$) but sensitive at gen-0/gen-1 ($|\\rho|{=}0.40$); tail decay is \\emph{weakly stationary}, passing TOST for only 4/8 generators ($|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$).\nEntropy std ($d{=}0.55$, $|\\rho|{=}0.16$) is the sole intermediate member.\n\\paragraph{Validation.}\nPer-generator split analysis provides the strongest validation: each of the eight hold-out generators independently recovers the identical drifting/non-drifting partition (per-generator silhouette range 0.505--0.760; Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\n$k{=}2$ clustering in standardized $(|\\rho|, d)$ space corroborates this separation (silhouette\\,=\\,0.660), invariant across threshold choices $\\varepsilon \\in [0.10, 0.75]$.\nPer-generator TOST equivalence testing ($\\varepsilon{=}|d|{<}0.20$, following Cohen's~\\citeyearpar{cohen1988statistical} conventional threshold; gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) confirms near-stationarity for entropy min (8/8 pass) and entropy max (6/8); tail decay passes for only 4/8, with $|d|{=}0.10$--$0.22$ spanning the boundary.\nA permutation test ($n{=}10{,}000$) establishes the three-vs-four split as non-random ($p{=}0.030$; fixed-label silhouette\\,=\\,0.660 vs.\\ null mean\\,=\\,0.46); exhaustive enumeration of all $\\binom{7}{3}{=}35$ possible splits ranks this partition first (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-validation}).\nReclassifying either boundary feature as intermediate preserves the core drifting/non-drifting split and leaves classification unchanged.\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThe full 7-feature set (F1\\,=\\,0.773, 10-fold stratified CV) outperforms surface-only (0.688) and reference-only (0.713; McNemar $p \\approx 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation-table}).\nPCA confirms the three drifting features share a single latent dimension (92.3\\% variance; Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-feature-correlation}); replacing them with PC1 costs only 1.1\\,pp, reinforcing that DDR's contribution is identifying \\emph{which} distributional properties drift, not classification per se.\nTTR and hapax ratio ($r = 0.97$) are both retained for marginal LOFO gains despite feature masking \\cite{strobl_conditional_2008} (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}).\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family reference models.}\nAlternative references (OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-0.6B) achieve comparable LOGO F1 (spread 0.013; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-ref}), bounding the same-family affinity effect; depth features are robust to reference model choice, though residual same-family proximity may inflate Pythia-family F1 by up to 1.3\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Temperature and prompt-type sensitivity.}\nAll four tested generators degrade at $t{=}0.9$ ($-9.3$ to $-26.0$\\,pp binary F1); low-temperature improvement is family-dependent ($+7$--$9$\\,pp for Pythia/OLMo at $t{=}0.5$, negligible for Qwen; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}).\nContinuation chains show the strongest drift (F1\\,=\\,0.781), with cross-prompt-type transfer confirming shared signal (LOPTO F1\\,=\\,0.753; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}); the DDR partition hierarchy is preserved across temperatures despite absolute effect-size compression (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Comparison with Detection Baselines}\n\\label{sec:results-baselines}\n\nExisting detection methods target binary real-vs-synthetic classification; we test whether their continuous scores incidentally encode recursive depth by repurposing four detection scores as ordinal depth predictors under identical XGBoost and LOGO conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{Feat.} & \\textbf{3-cl F1} & \\textbf{Bin F1} & \\textbf{ms/text} \\\\\n\\midrule\n4-det.\\ + surface     & 6 & \\textbf{0.795} & \\textbf{0.708} & 22.8 \\\\\n4-detector ens.\\      & 4 & 0.785 & 0.700 & 22.8 \\\\\n7-feature (ours)      & 7 & 0.774 & 0.705 & \\textbf{9.7} \\\\\n\\midrule\nAvg-LogProb           & 1 & 0.707 & 0.673 & --- \\\\\nFastDetectGPT         & 1 & 0.660 & 0.569 & --- \\\\\nLD-Score              & 1 & 0.630 & 0.675 & --- \\\\\nBinoculars            & 1 & 0.411 & 0.539 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Depth classification (8-gen LOGO, pooled evaluation; pooled F1 is slightly higher than per-generator macro F1 in Table~\\ref{tab:logo-results} because larger test folds from high-performing generators contribute proportionally more). Identical XGBoost configuration. 7-feature: one 410M model (905\\,MB); 4-det ensembles: two models, 1.4B params (2{,}868\\,MB). Individual detection scores (N/A) share the reference-model forward pass; per-method timing is not separately profiled.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_baseline_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{LOGO binary F1 vs.\\ inference cost in ms/text (8-gen pooled). The 7-feature ensemble (one 410M reference model) matches multi-detector ensembles (two models, 1.4B total) at 2.4$\\times$ lower cost; individual detectors vary widely when repurposed for depth. Inference costs measured on a single NVIDIA RTX 5090.}\n\\label{fig:baseline-ci}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble surpasses all individual baselines in 3-class F1 ($\\Delta$\\,=\\,6.7--36.3\\,pp; Figure~\\ref{fig:baseline-ci}) and the pure 4-detector ensemble in binary F1 (0.705 vs.\\ 0.700).\nAdding DDR's surface features to the four detectors yields the highest binary F1 (0.708), marginally above the 7-feature set at 2.4$\\times$ the inference cost.\nThis result underscores that DDR's primary contribution is not classification performance (multi-detector ensembles match or marginally exceed it) but the decomposition of depth signal into location-sensitive and shape-stable components, a structural distinction absent from both binary detection methods and model collapse analyses (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n",
      "numLines": 142,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 142
    }
  }
}

1	\subsection{9-Generator Scale Evaluation}\label{app:9gen-scale}
2	
3	This analysis extends the 8-generator LOGO evaluation to nine generators by adding Gemma-2-2B, a model from a previously unrepresented architecture family (Gemma).
4	All results use class-balanced LOGO; the 8-gen averages here (0.770/0.701) differ slightly from Table~\ref{tab:logo-results} (0.762/0.694), which uses an unfiltered protocol.
5	Table~\ref{tab:9gen-logo} reports per-generator LOGO performance under this expanded set.
6

32	
33	\paragraph{Overall comparison.}
34	Adding Gemma-2-2B reduces the mean 3-class LOGO F1 from 0.770 (8-gen) to 0.751 (9-gen), a drop of $\Delta = -0.019$.
35	The per-generator F1 values for the original eight generators shift by at most $[-0.011, +0.002]$, indicating that the inclusion of Gemma data in training does not destabilize existing generalization patterns.
36	
37	\paragraph{Gemma-2-2B anomaly.}
38	Gemma-2-2B is the weakest held-out generator: 3-class F1\,=\,0.628, binary F1\,=\,0.440.
39	Per-class breakdown reveals a striking asymmetry: gen-0 F1\,=\,0.991 and gen-2+ F1\,=\,0.856, but gen-1 F1\,=\,0.036; the classifier almost entirely fails to identify first-generation Gemma continuations.
40	This is not a data-quality artifact: the Gemma-2-2B partition contains 4{,}957 instances with a balanced depth distribution ($\sim$990 per level).
41	A plausible explanation is that Gemma-2's first-generation continuations occupy a region of feature space highly similar to human-written text (gen-0), causing systematic misclassification of gen-1 as gen-0 or gen-2+.
42	This constitutes an architecture-specific generalization limitation rather than a systemic failure of the depth fingerprinting approach.
43	Several architectural differences may explain this behavior: (a)~Gemma-2's distinct attention mechanism and SentencePiece tokenizer with a larger vocabulary may produce first-generation continuations whose feature-space signature closely resembles human text, causing systematic gen-0/gen-1 confusion; (b)~this anomaly does not affect DDR's validity for the remaining eight generators, where per-generator F1 shifts by at most 0.011 upon Gemma inclusion.
44	
45	\paragraph{Scale effect.}
46	A clear parameter-count gradient emerges across the nine generators: three of four sub-1.7B generators achieve F1\,$>$\,0.81 (OLMo-2-1B at 0.773 is the exception), mid-range models (2--3B) cluster at 0.63--0.81, and large models (7B+) fall to 0.60--0.70.
47	Qwen3-8B (8.2B parameters) achieves 3-class F1\,=\,0.696 under the 9-gen split, compared to 0.701 in the class-balanced 8-gen evaluation; this small difference reflects the changed training composition rather than a property of the Qwen3-8B generator itself.
48	
49	\paragraph{14B-parameter evidence.}
50	Preliminary evidence from a 14.7B generator (Qwen3-14B-Base) confirms that the DDR partition persists at larger scale: Cohen's $d$ for drifting features remains in the large-effect range ($d{=}0.78$--$1.12$), though Spearman $|\rho|$ attenuates by 21--35\%, with entropy mean falling just below the drifting threshold ($|\rho|{=}0.39 < 0.40$, $d{=}0.78$).
51	This scale-dependent signal compression is consistent with larger models producing more self-typical outputs that narrow inter-depth contrasts; the partition structure is preserved even as absolute effect sizes compress.
52	
```

1	This appendix visualizes per-feature convergence trajectories across generation depth, complementing the DDR analysis in \S\ref{sec:results-differential}.
2	
3	\begin{figure}[H]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_convergence_dynamics.pdf}
6	\caption{Per-feature convergence dynamics across generation depths 0--4, averaged over 8 generators. Each feature is expressed as Cohen's $d$ relative to depth~0, so all features start at zero and the $y$-axis directly reflects effect size. (a)~Drifting features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) and the intermediate feature (entropy std) diverge monotonically; lexical diversity features (TTR, hapax ratio) form a fast-convergence group ($|d| > 2$ by depth~2), while entropy mean and entropy std drift more slowly. (b)~Near-stationary features (entropy max, tail decay, entropy min) remain within $|d| < 0.25$ across all depths, confirming location stability. Shaded bands indicate $\pm 1$ standard deviation across generators.}
7	\label{fig:convergence-dynamics}
8	\end{figure}
9

1	We assess robustness to post-generation text modification by randomly replacing words at varying rates and re-evaluating the 8-generator LOGO pipeline (Table~\ref{tab:human-edit}).
2	Random word replacement represents a worst-case perturbation: real human editing preserves semantic coherence, so practical degradation is expected to be substantially lower than these estimates.
3	
4	\begin{table}[ht]
5	\centering
6	\small
7	\caption{Robustness to random word replacement under 8-gen LOGO evaluation (binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Bootstrap 95\% CIs on binary F1 ($n$\,=\,1000).}
8	\label{tab:human-edit}
9	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
10	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
11	\toprule
12	\textbf{Perturbation rate} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{3-class F1} \\
13	\midrule
14	0\% (baseline) & 0.695 & [0.680, 0.710] & 0.737 \\
15	5\%            & 0.650 & [0.635, 0.666] & 0.641 \\
16	10\%           & 0.614 & [0.599, 0.629] & 0.492 \\
17	20\%           & 0.591 & [0.576, 0.605] & 0.367 \\
18	\bottomrule
19	\end{tabular}}
20	\end{table}
21	
22	Binary depth discrimination (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) degrades sublinearly: the first 5\% replacement causes the largest drop ($-$4.6\,pp), with diminishing marginal impact thereafter ($-$3.6\,pp for 5\%$\to$10\%, $-$2.3\,pp for 10\%$\to$20\%).
23	At 10\% replacement, binary F1 remains 0.614, well above the 0.50 random baseline, which indicates that depth signal survives moderate perturbation.
24	
25	Three-class classification is more sensitive (0.737$\to$0.367 at 20\%), because distinguishing gen-0 from gen-1 relies heavily on lexical diversity features (TTR, hapax ratio) that are directly disrupted by word replacement.
26	The binary metric, which excludes gen-0, is inherently more robust since it depends primarily on reference-model entropy features distributed across many tokens.
27	
28	\paragraph{Feature subset degradation.}
29	Table~\ref{tab:feature-degrade} compares perturbation robustness of the full 7-feature ensemble against an entropy-only subset (5 reference-model features).
30	The higher unperturbed binary F1 of the entropy-only subset (0.833 vs.\ 0.695) reflects the strong discriminability of reference-model features for the coarse binary task (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+); however, lexical features are essential for the finer-grained ordinal task (3-class F1: 0.737 vs.\ 0.688) and provide greater robustness under perturbation.
31	At 20\% replacement, the 7-feature ensemble surpasses entropy-only on binary F1 (0.591 vs.\ 0.569), despite starting from a lower baseline.
32	Random word replacement drastically alters per-token context and makes entropy features (computed over the full token sequence) extremely sensitive to noise.
33	Lexical features (TTR, hapax ratio), as document-level summary statistics, are more stable under per-word perturbation and act as stabilizers in the full ensemble.
34	The crossover confirms that degradation in the full feature set reflects genuine signal disruption rather than a lexical-feature artifact.
35	
36	\begin{table}[ht]
37	\centering
38	\small
39	\caption{Feature subset degradation under random word replacement (8-gen LOGO). Entropy-only: 5 reference-model features; 7-feature: full ensemble including lexical features.}
40	\label{tab:feature-degrade}
41	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
42	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
43	\toprule
44	\textbf{Perturb.} & \textbf{Ent-only Bin} & \textbf{7-feat Bin} & \textbf{Ent-only 3-cl} & \textbf{7-feat 3-cl} \\
45	\midrule
46	0\%  & 0.833 & 0.695 & 0.688 & 0.737 \\
47	5\%  & 0.738 & 0.650 & 0.539 & 0.641 \\
48	10\% & 0.667 & 0.614 & 0.427 & 0.492 \\
49	20\% & 0.569 & 0.591 & 0.314 & 0.367 \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}}
52	\end{table}
53	
54	\paragraph{Baseline comparison.}
55	Table~\ref{tab:baseline-robustness} compares robustness across detection methods under identical perturbation conditions (8-gen LOGO, binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+).
56	
57	\begin{table}[ht]
58	\centering
59	\small
60	\caption{Baseline robustness under random word replacement (8-gen LOGO, binary F1). Bold indicates highest F1 at each perturbation rate.}
61	\label{tab:baseline-robustness}
62	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
63	\toprule
64	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{0\%} & \textbf{5\%} & \textbf{10\%} & \textbf{20\%} \\
65	\midrule
66	7-feature ensemble & \textbf{0.695} & 0.650 & 0.614 & 0.591 \\
67	LD-Score           & 0.677 & \textbf{0.673} & \textbf{0.677} & \textbf{0.671} \\
68	Avg-LogProb        & 0.682 & 0.419 & 0.335 & 0.375 \\
69	FastDetectGPT      & 0.571 & 0.437 & 0.468 & 0.514 \\
70	Binoculars\textsuperscript{\dag}         & 0.500 & 0.461 & 0.446 & 0.425 \\
71	\bottomrule
72	\end{tabular}
73	
74	\vspace{2pt}
75	{\footnotesize \textsuperscript{\dag}Binoculars operates at chance level on this binary task; its degradation figures are not meaningful.}
76	\end{table}
77	
78	The 7-feature ensemble achieves the highest unperturbed binary F1 (0.695), but LD-Score surpasses it under perturbation due to near-zero degradation ($-$0.6\,pp across the full 0\%$\to$20\% range).
79	FastDetectGPT and Avg-LogProb exhibit non-monotonic degradation at higher perturbation rates, likely because severe perturbation introduces distributional artifacts that these token-level methods detect as anomalous.
80	Avg-LogProb is the most fragile overall ($-$34.7\,pp at 10\%), while FastDetectGPT drops sharply at 5\% ($-$13.4\,pp) before partially recovering.
81	LD-Score is nearly invariant to perturbation, consistent with its reliance on corpus-level distributional divergence rather than per-token likelihoods.
82	The 7-feature ensemble offers the highest starting performance among single-reference methods, with sublinear degradation ($-$10.4\,pp at 20\%).
83	
84	\paragraph{LLM-simulated sentence regeneration.}
85	To test robustness under semantically coherent editing, we use Qwen3-0.6B-Base to randomly regenerate 1--3 sentences per passage (avg 1.69 sentences, ${\sim}$50\% edit distance) and re-evaluate the 8-gen LOGO pipeline.
86	Binary F1 drops from 0.692 to 0.647 ($-$4.5\,pp, 6.5\% relative); 3-class F1 drops from 0.739 to 0.667 ($-$7.1\,pp, 9.7\% relative).
87	The binary degradation ($-$4.5\,pp) is nearly identical to that caused by 5\% random word replacement ($-$4.6\,pp), despite the LLM rephrase altering ${\sim}$50\% of characters.
88	This asymmetry arises because LLM-generated replacements preserve the underlying statistical signature of the generation process (entropy dynamics, depth-accumulated distributional drift), whereas random word substitution directly destroys local statistical signals.
89	Depth profiling thus captures deep generative structure that survives semantic-preserving edits.
90	
91	\paragraph{Seed complexity.}
92	Performance is stable across seed text complexity: stratifying seeds by vocabulary richness (TTR terciles) yields maximum F1 variation of 1.1\,pp across groups.
93	
```

1	% section header and label in main.tex
2	
3	\begin{figure}[h!]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_nll_distribution.pdf}
6	\caption{Token-level NLL distribution by generation depth, aggregated across all 8 generators (Pythia-410M reference model). Dashed lines indicate median NLL per depth. The systematic leftward shift demonstrates that deeper-generation text is increasingly predictable.}
7	\label{fig:nll-dist}
8	\end{figure}
9	
10	To visualize how the reference-model surprisal landscape shifts with generation depth, we plot the kernel density estimate of per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) across depths~0--4 in Figure~\ref{fig:nll-dist}.
11	For each of the 8 base generators, we sample 25 texts per depth (1{,}000 texts total, $\sim$236k tokens) and compute token-level NLL under the Pythia-410M reference model.
12	The distributions exhibit a pronounced leftward shift as depth increases: gen-0 text (human-written seed) has a median NLL of 1.64~nats, dropping to 0.96 at depth~1 and reaching 0.02 at depth~4, confirming that recursively generated text becomes increasingly predictable to the reference model.
13	The monotonic compression of the NLL distribution provides a mechanistic explanation for the entropy-based features that drive our depth classifier.
14	
```

```text
1	\section{Per-Generator Equivalence Testing}
2	\label{app:tost}
3	
4	To confirm that the near-stationary classification is not a pooling artifact, we apply two one-sided $t$-tests (TOST) \cite{lakens_equivalence} for each feature within each generator separately (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, equivalence bound $|d| < 0.20$).
5	We adopt Cohen's conventional small effect size ($d{=}0.20$) \cite{cohen1988statistical} as the smallest effect of practical significance (SESOI): effects below this threshold are unlikely to be practically meaningful for depth discrimination.
6	LOFO analysis independently supports this choice: removing any near-stationary feature changes classification F1 by $\leq$1.5\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}), linking the statistical threshold to classification irrelevance.
7	Sensitivity analysis at $\varepsilon \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25\}$ confirms the partition is robust to the bound choice (see DDR Validation appendix).
8	Table~\ref{tab:tost-per-gen} reports observed $|d|$; \checkmark{} marks features for which the TOST null hypothesis (non-equivalence) is rejected at $\alpha = 0.05$.
9	
10	Entropy min passes equivalence for all eight generators; entropy max passes for six of eight (exceeding $\varepsilon$ only for Pythia-1B and Pythia-2.8B, where $|d| \approx 0.21$--$0.25$); tail decay passes for four of eight.
11	All drifting and intermediate features fail equivalence uniformly, with $|d|$ ranging from 0.23 (entropy std, Mistral) to 1.40 (entropy mean, Pythia-1B).
12	The partition is robust at the individual-generator level, with entropy min as the most consistently stationary feature.
13	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-scatter} visualizes the DDR partition in ($|\rho|$, $d$) space, showing a clear gap between the three groups.
14

      "content": "\\section{Per-Generator Equivalence Testing}\n\\label{app:tost}\n\nTo confirm that the near-stationary classification is not a pooling artifact, we apply two one-sided $t$-tests (TOST) \\cite{lakens_equivalence} for each feature within each generator separately (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, equivalence bound $|d| < 0.20$).\nWe adopt Cohen's conventional small effect size ($d{=}0.20$) \\cite{cohen1988statistical} as the smallest effect of practical significance (SESOI): effects below this threshold are unlikely to be practically meaningful for depth discrimination.\nLOFO analysis independently supports this choice: removing any near-stationary feature changes classification F1 by $\\leq$1.5\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}), linking the statistical threshold to classification irrelevance.\nSensitivity analysis at $\\varepsilon \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25\\}$ confirms the partition is robust to the bound choice (see DDR Validation appendix).\nTable~\\ref{tab:tost-per-gen} reports observed $|d|$; \\checkmark{} marks features for which the TOST null hypothesis (non-equivalence) is rejected at $\\alpha = 0.05$.\n\nEntropy min passes equivalence for all eight generators; entropy max passes for six of eight (exceeding $\\varepsilon$ only for Pythia-1B and Pythia-2.8B, where $|d| \\approx 0.21$--$0.25$); tail decay passes for four of eight.\nAll drifting and intermediate features fail equivalence uniformly, with $|d|$ ranging from 0.23 (entropy std, Mistral) to 1.40 (entropy mean, Pythia-1B).\nThe partition is robust at the individual-generator level, with entropy min as the most consistently stationary feature.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ddr-scatter} visualizes the DDR partition in ($|\\rho|$, $d$) space, showing a clear gap between the three groups.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Per-generator TOST equivalence ($\\varepsilon{=}|d|{<}0.20$, gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+). ```text
1	\subsection*{Data-Driven Clustering}
2	
3	To assess the DDR partition structure and determine whether the drifting/non-drifting split is data-driven, we apply $k$-means clustering ($k \in \{2,3,4,5\}$) to the seven features in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space (Table~\ref{tab:clustering}).
4	
5	\begin{table}[ht]
6	\centering
7	\small
8	\caption{$k$-means clustering of 7 features in $(|\rho|, d)$ space.}
9	\label{tab:clustering}
10	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
11	\begin{tabular}{ccp{5.5cm}}
12	\toprule
13	$k$ & Silhouette & Clusters \\
14	\midrule
15	2 & \textbf{0.660} & \{$\bar{H}$, TTR, hapax\} $\mid$ \{$\sigma_H$, $H_{\min}$, $H_{\max}$, tail\} \\
16	3 & 0.532 & \{$\bar{H}$, TTR, hapax\} $\mid$ \{$\sigma_H$, $H_{\max}$, tail\} $\mid$ \{$H_{\min}$\} \\
17	4 & 0.527 & \{$\bar{H}$, TTR, hapax\} $\mid$ \{$H_{\max}$, tail\} $\mid$ \{$\sigma_H$\} $\mid$ \{$H_{\min}$\} \\
18	\bottomrule
19	\end{tabular}}
20	\end{table}
21	
22	The primary drifting/non-drifting split ($k{=}2$, silhouette\,=\,0.660) is the strongest cluster structure.
23	At $k{=}3$, entropy min forms its own cluster rather than joining the near-stationary group because its pooled $|\rho|{=}0.40$ (driven by the gen-0/gen-1 boundary) places it far from other near-stationary features in $\rho$ space.
24	The 3/1/3 DDR partition resolves this by using the gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+ Cohen's $d$ ($d{=}0.03$) to place entropy min in the near-stationary group, a domain-informed refinement that unsupervised clustering cannot recover from pooled statistics alone.
25	At $k{=}4$ (silhouette\,=\,0.571), entropy std separates from the near-stationary group, consistent with its intermediate DDR classification.
26	
27	\paragraph{Exhaustive partition enumeration.}
28	To confirm that the three-vs-four split is optimal rather than incidental, we enumerate all $\binom{7}{3}{=}35$ possible ways to assign three features to a ``drifting'' group and the remaining four to ``non-drifting,'' computing the silhouette score for each partition in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space.
29	The observed DDR partition (\{entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio\}) achieves the highest silhouette (0.660), with a gap of 0.18 to the second-ranked split; 20 of 35 partitions produce negative silhouette scores.
30	
```

30	
31	\paragraph{Per-generator replication.}
32	To rule out the possibility that the pooled silhouette is an artifact of mixing heterogeneous generators, we repeat the $(|\rho|, d)$ clustering independently for each of the eight generators.
33	Every generator recovers the identical \{$\bar{H}$, TTR, hapax\} vs.\ \{$\sigma_H$, $H_{\min}$, $H_{\max}$, tail\} partition, with silhouette scores ranging from 0.505 (Pythia-1B) to 0.760 (Mistral-7B-v0.3; mean\,=\,0.651).
34	No generator falls below the 0.50 moderate-structure threshold, confirming that the two-cluster structure is a per-generator property rather than a pooling artifact.
35	
36	\subsection*{TOST Equivalence Bound Sensitivity}
37	\label{app:tost-sensitivity}
38	
39	Table~\ref{tab:tost-sensitivity} shows the fraction of generators for which each feature passes the TOST equivalence test (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, $\alpha{=}0.05$) at three equivalence bounds $\varepsilon$.
40	
41	\begin{table}[ht]
42	\centering
43	\small
44	\caption{TOST equivalence pass rate across 8 generators at varying $\varepsilon$.}
45	\label{tab:tost-sensitivity}
46	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
47	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
48	\toprule
49	\textbf{DDR Zone} & \textbf{Feature} & $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon{=}0.15}$ & $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon{=}0.20}$ & $\boldsymbol{\varepsilon{=}0.25}$ \\
50	\midrule
51	\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & $\bar{H}$ (ent.\ mean) & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\
52	 & TTR & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\
53	 & hapax ratio & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\
54	\midrule
55	Intermediate & $\sigma_H$ (ent.\ std) & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\
56	\midrule
57	\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & $H_{\min}$ (ent.\ min) & 7/8 & 8/8 & 8/8 \\
58	 & $H_{\max}$ (ent.\ max) & 6/8 & 6/8 & 6/8 \\
59	 & tail decay & 4/8 & 4/8 & 7/8 \\
60	\bottomrule
61	\end{tabular}}
62	\end{table}
63	
64	The partition is robust to the equivalence bound: drifting features reject equivalence at all $\varepsilon$ for all generators, while entropy min passes even at the stricter bound $\varepsilon{=}0.15$ (7/8 generators).
65	Tail decay is the most $\varepsilon$-sensitive near-stationary feature, passing 4/8 at $\varepsilon{=}0.20$ but 7/8 at $\varepsilon{=}0.25$; the four non-passing generators show $|d|{=}0.15$--$0.22$, still well below the drifting threshold ($d > 0.50$).
66	Entropy max is stable across all three bounds (6/8), with the two failures (Pythia-1B $d{=}0.21$, Pythia-2.8B $d{=}0.25$) reflecting same-family reference affinity with the Pythia-410M reference model.
67	
68	\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.}
69	To address concerns about the sensitivity of the drifting-boundary threshold,
70	we sweep $|d|$ from $0.10$ to $0.80$ in increments of $0.05$ and record the
71	resulting binary partition (drifting vs.\ stationary) along with the silhouette
72	score in standardized $(|\rho|, d)$ space.
73	Across the entire range, only \emph{three} distinct partitions emerge,
74	reflecting natural gaps between feature clusters rather than threshold artifacts.
75	The three-feature drifting set (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio)
76	is stable across $|d| \in [0.50,\, 0.75]$ with a silhouette of $0.660$---the
77	peak value in the sweep.
78	Including the intermediate feature entropy std ($d{=}0.49$) yields a
79	4/3 partition stable across $|d| \in [0.10,\, 0.45]$ (silhouette\,=\,0.451).
80	The partition used in our analysis corresponds to the peak-silhouette region,
81	confirming that the drifting/stationary boundary reflects a natural gap in the
82	$(|\rho|, d)$ landscape rather than an artifact of a particular
83	threshold choice.
84	
85	\paragraph{Partition stability across $\varepsilon$ thresholds.}
86	\label{app:epsilon-sweep}
87	
88	The DDR partition classifies features as non-stationary ($d \geq \varepsilon$) or stationary ($d < \varepsilon$) based on pooled Cohen's $d$ (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
89	Table~\ref{tab:epsilon-sweep} reports the critical $\varepsilon$ at which each feature's classification changes; the three drifting and three near-stationary features maintain their classification for all $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$.
90	
91	\begin{table}[ht]
92	\centering
93	\small
94	\caption{Partition stability under varying $\varepsilon$. Critical threshold: the $\varepsilon$ above which a feature is reclassified as stationary. The drifting/near-stationary split is invariant for $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$; only entropy std ($d{=}0.55$) changes at $\varepsilon > 0.55$.}
95	\label{tab:epsilon-sweep}
96	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
97	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
98	\toprule
99	\textbf{DDR Zone} & \textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Pooled $d$} & \textbf{Stationary at $\varepsilon >$} \\
100	\midrule
101	\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & $\bar{H}$ (ent.\ mean) & 1.17 & 1.17 \\
102	 & TTR & 0.88 & 0.88 \\
103	 & hapax ratio & 0.76 & 0.76 \\
104	\midrule
105	Intermediate & $\sigma_H$ (ent.\ std) & 0.55 & 0.55 \\
106	\midrule
107	\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & $H_{\max}$ (ent.\ max) & 0.08 & 0.08 \\
108	 & tail decay & 0.08 & 0.08 \\
109	 & $H_{\min}$ (ent.\ min) & 0.016 & 0.016 \\
110	\bottomrule
111	\end{tabular}}
112	\end{table}
113	
114	\noindent The 3/1/3 partition is stable across $\varepsilon \in [0.10, 0.75]$: the three drifting features remain non-stationary at all $\varepsilon < 0.76$, the three near-stationary features remain stationary at all $\varepsilon > 0.08$, and only entropy std changes classification (becoming stationary at $\varepsilon > 0.55$), confirming it as the sole threshold-sensitive feature.
115	The minimum gap between adjacent zone boundaries ($d_{\text{hapax}} - d_{\sigma_H} = 0.21$) exceeds typical effect-size estimation uncertainty, providing a margin against sampling noise.
116	
117	\subsection*{Holdout Generator Split Validation}
118	\label{app:holdout-split}
119	
120	To confirm the DDR partition is not an artifact of the specific generator pool, we split the eight generators into disjoint selection (4) and validation (4) sets, compute per-subset pooled $(|\rho|, d)$, and check whether all seven features receive the same zone assignment in both subsets.
121	We evaluate two complementary splits to avoid dependence on a single partition:
122	Split~A selects \{Pythia-2.8B, Qwen3-1.7B, OLMo, Mistral\} (validation: Pythia-1B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B, Llama);
123	Split~B selects \{Pythia-1B, Qwen3-0.6B, OLMo, Mistral\} (validation: Pythia-2.8B, Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-8B, Llama).
124	
125	\begin{table}[ht]
126	\centering
127	\small
128	\caption{Holdout split validation of the DDR partition. Selection (sel) and validation (val) $|\rho|$ and $d$ for two disjoint 4/4 generator splits. Zone assignment is identical across all four subsets for every feature.}
129	\label{tab:holdout-split}
130	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
131	\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}
132	\toprule
133	& & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{Split A}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{Split B}} \\
134	\cmidrule(lr){3-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-8}
135	\textbf{Zone} & \textbf{Feature} & sel $|\rho|$ & val $|\rho|$ & $\Delta|\rho|$ & sel $|\rho|$ & val $|\rho|$ & $\Delta|\rho|$ \\
136	\midrule
137	\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & $\bar{H}$ (ent.\ mean) & 0.60 & 0.58 & 0.02 & 0.60 & 0.58 & 0.02 \\
138	 & TTR & 0.61 & 0.59 & 0.02 & 0.60 & 0.59 & 0.01 \\
139	 & hapax ratio & 0.62 & 0.60 & 0.02 & 0.62 & 0.61 & 0.01 \\
140	\midrule
141	Intermediate & $\sigma_H$ (ent.\ std) & 0.19 & 0.21 & 0.02 & 0.22 & 0.17 & 0.05 \\
142	\midrule
143	\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & $H_{\min}$ (ent.\ min) & 0.39 & 0.36 & 0.03 & 0.38 & 0.37 & 0.01 \\
144	 & $H_{\max}$ (ent.\ max) & 0.05 & 0.05 & 0.00 & 0.04 & 0.06 & 0.02 \\
145	 & tail decay & 0.10 & 0.08 & 0.02 & 0.11 & 0.07 & 0.04 \\
146	\bottomrule
147	\end{tabular}}
148	\end{table}
149	
150	Under simple joint $(|\rho|, d)$ thresholds, both splits produce a 3/2/2 partition: entropy min is classified as a second intermediate feature due to its elevated pooled $|\rho|$ (0.36--0.39).
151	However, entropy min's binary (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) $d$ values are uniformly below 0.06 across all four split${\times}$subset combinations, consistent with near-stationarity on the task-relevant metric; its pooled $|\rho|$ reflects change concentrated at the gen-0/gen-1 boundary rather than sustained drift.
152	Five of seven features are unambiguously classified under any reasonable criterion; entropy min's boundary status is resolved by the binary-task $d$ criterion.
153	
154	Selection and validation subsets produce identical partitions in both splits (Table~\ref{tab:holdout-split}; Figure~\ref{fig:holdout-scatter}).
155	The maximum $\Delta|\rho|$ between selection and validation is 0.05 (entropy std, Split~B), and the minimum gap between adjacent zones remains $>$0.15 in all subsets.
156	
157	\begin{figure}[H]
158	\centering
159	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{fig_ddr_holdout_split.pdf}
160	\caption{Holdout split validation in $(|\rho|, d)$ space. Filled markers: selection set; hollow: validation set. Zone assignments are identical across subsets; selection/validation pairs cluster tightly for all features.}
161	\label{fig:holdout-scatter}
162	\end{figure}
163	
164	\subsection*{Heteroscedasticity Sensitivity}
165	\label{app:heteroscedasticity}
166	
167	Levene tests reject equal variance between gen-1 and gen-2+ for drifting features ($p < 10^{-10}$; variance ratios 1.86--2.21).
168	To verify that the DDR partition is robust to this heteroscedasticity, we supplement pooled Cohen's $d$ with Glass's $\Delta$ (using the gen-1 standard deviation as denominator) and Cliff's $\delta$ (a non-parametric ordinal effect size; Table~\ref{tab:effect-sizes}).
169	
170	\begin{table}[ht]
171	\centering
172	\small
173	\caption{Effect size comparison (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, $N$\,=\,29{,}095). Glass's $\Delta$ uses the gen-1 SD as denominator; Cliff's $\delta$ thresholds follow Romano et al.\ (2006): $|\delta| < 0.147$ negligible, $< 0.33$ small, $< 0.474$ medium, otherwise large.}
174	\label{tab:effect-sizes}
175	\begin{tabular}{llrrr}
176	\toprule
177	\textbf{Zone} & \textbf{Feature} & \textbf{$|d|$} & \textbf{$|\Delta|$} & \textbf{$|\delta|$} \\
178	\midrule
179	\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & ent.\ mean & 1.054 & 1.410 & 0.587 \\
180	 & TTR & 0.814 & 1.116 & 0.510 \\
181	 & hapax ratio & 0.735 & 0.936 & 0.528 \\
182	\midrule
183	Intermediate & ent.\ std & 0.457 & 0.605 & 0.295 \\
184	\midrule
185	\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & tail decay & 0.087 & 0.099 & 0.072 \\
186	 & ent.\ max & 0.062 & 0.065 & 0.021 \\
187	 & ent.\ min & 0.016 & 0.015 & 0.354$^{\dagger}$ \\
188	\bottomrule
189	\end{tabular}
190	\end{table}
191	
192	\noindent Effect sizes in Table~\ref{tab:effect-sizes} are computed on the binary task (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+); the main text (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) reports 3-class pooled $d$ values (e.g., hapax ratio $d{=}0.76$), which are slightly larger because the gen-0/gen-1 contrast contributes additional separation.
193	Glass's $\Delta$ amplifies drifting effects (1.41 vs.\ 1.05 for entropy mean) because gen-2+ variance exceeds gen-1 variance (ratio 2.08), making the pooled Cohen's $d$ conservative.
194	Near-stationary features remain negligible under both parametric metrics ($|\Delta| \leq 0.099$).
195	The minimum drifting-to-maximum-near-stationary gap is $9.5\times$ under Glass's $\Delta$ (0.936\,/\,0.099); heteroscedasticity does not erode the partition.
196	Cliff's $\delta$ classifies all drifting features as ``large'' ($|\delta| > 0.474$) and entropy std as ``small'' ($|\delta|{=}0.295$), consistent with the parametric partition.
197	
198	\noindent $^{\dagger}$Entropy min's elevated Cliff's $\delta$ (0.354, ``medium'') reflects distributional shape asymmetry between gen-1 and gen-2+ that rank-based statistics detect but location measures do not (Cohen's $d{=}0.016$, Glass's $\Delta{=}0.015$).
199	This is consistent with its documented boundary status (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}): the gen-0/gen-1 transition produces rank-order shifts that persist into deeper generations without affecting the mean.

mean\\,=\\,0.651).\nNo generator falls below the 0.50 moderate-structure threshold, confirming that the two-cluster structure is a per-generator property rather than a pooling artifact.\n\n\\subsection*{TOST Equivalence Bound Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:tost-sensitivity}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:tost-sensitivity} shows the fraction of generators for which each feature passes the TOST equivalence test (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, $\\alpha{=}0.05$) at three equivalence bounds $\\varepsilon$.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{TOST equivalence pass rate across 8 generators at varying $\\varepsilon$.}\n\\label{tab:tost-sensitivity}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{DDR Zone} & \\textbf{Feature} & $\\boldsymbol{\\varepsilon{=}0.15}$ & $\\boldsymbol{\\varepsilon{=}0.20}$ & $\\boldsymbol{\\varepsilon{=}0.25}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & $\\bar{H}$ (ent.\\ mean) & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\\\\n & TTR & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\\\\n & hapax ratio & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\\\\n\\midrule\nIntermediate & $\\sigma_H$ (ent.\\ std) & 0/8 & 0/8 & 0/8 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & $H_{\\min}$ (ent.\\ min) & 7/8 & 8/8 & 8/8 \\\\\n & $H_{\\max}$ (ent.\\ max) & 6/8 & 6/8 & 6/8 \\\\\n & tail decay & 4/8 & 4/8 & 7/8 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe partition is robust to the equivalence bound: drifting features reject equivalence at all $\\varepsilon$ for all generators, while entropy min passes even at the stricter bound $\\varepsilon{=}0.15$ (7/8 generators).\nTail decay is the most $\\varepsilon$-sensitive near-stationary feature, passing 4/8 at $\\varepsilon{=}0.20$ but 7/8 at $\\varepsilon{=}0.25$; the four non-passing generators show $|d|{=}0.15$--$0.22$, still well below the drifting threshold ($d > 0.50$).\nEntropy max is stable across all three bounds (6/8), with the two failures (Pythia-1B $d{=}0.21$, Pythia-2.8B $d{=}0.25$) reflecting same-family reference affinity with the Pythia-410M reference model.\n\n\\paragraph{Threshold sensitivity.}\nTo address concerns about the sensitivity of the drifting-boundary threshold,\nwe sweep $|d|$ from $0.10$ to $0.80$ in increments of $0.05$ and record the\nresulting binary partition (drifting vs.\\ stationary) along with the silhouette\nscore in standardized $(|\\rho|, d)$ space.\nAcross the entire range, only \\emph{three} distinct partitions emerge,\nreflecting natural gaps between feature clusters rather than threshold artifacts.\nThe three-feature drifting set (entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio)\nis stable across $|d| \\in [0.50,\\, 0.75]$ with a silhouette of $0.660$---the\npeak value in the sweep.\nIncluding the intermediate feature entropy std ($d{=}0.49$) yields a\n4/3 partition stable across $|d| \\in [0.10,\\, 0.45]$ (silhouette\\,=\\,0.451).\nThe partition used in our analysis corresponds to the peak-silhouette region,\nconfirming that the drifting/stationary boundary reflects a natural gap in the\n$(|\\rho|, d)$ landscape rather than an artifact of a particular\nthreshold choice.\n\n\\paragraph{Partition stability across $\\varepsilon$ thresholds.}\n\\label{app:epsilon-sweep}\n\nThe DDR partition classifies features as non-stationary ($d \\geq \\varepsilon$) or stationary ($d < \\varepsilon$) based on pooled Cohen's $d$ (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:epsilon-sweep} reports the critical $\\varepsilon$ at which each feature's classification changes; the three drifting and three near-stationary features maintain their classification for all $\\varepsilon \\in [0.10, 0.75]$.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Partition stability under varying $\\varepsilon$. Critical threshold: the $\\varepsilon$ above which a feature is reclassified as stationary. The drifting/near-stationary split is invariant for $\\varepsilon \\in [0.10, 0.75]$; only entropy std ($d{=}0.55$) changes at $\\varepsilon > 0.55$.}\n\\label{tab:epsilon-sweep}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{DDR Zone} & \\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{Pooled $d$} & \\textbf{Stationary at $\\varepsilon >$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & $\\bar{H}$ (ent.\\ mean) & 1.17 & 1.17 \\\\\n & TTR & 0.88 & 0.88 \\\\\n & hapax ratio & 0.76 & 0.76 \\\\\n\\midrule\nIntermediate & $\\sigma_H$ (ent.\\ std) & 0.55 & 0.55 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & $H_{\\max}$ (ent.\\ max) & 0.08 & 0.08 \\\\\n & tail decay & 0.08 & 0.08 \\\\\n & $H_{\\min}$ (ent.\\ min) & 0.016 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\noindent The 3/1/3 partition is stable across $\\varepsilon \\in [0.10, 0.75]$: the three drifting features remain non-stationary at all $\\varepsilon < 0.76$, the three near-stationary features remain stationary at all $\\varepsilon > 0.08$, and only entropy std changes classification (becoming stationary at $\\varepsilon > 0.55$), confirming it as the sole threshold-sensitive feature.\nThe minimum gap between adjacent zone boundaries ($d_{\\text{hapax}} - d_{\\sigma_H} = 0.21$) exceeds typical effect-size estimation uncertainty, providing a margin against sampling noise.\n\n\\subsection*{Holdout Generator Split Validation}\n\\label{app:holdout-split}\n\nTo confirm the DDR partition is not an artifact of the specific generator pool, we split the eight generators into disjoint selection (4) and validation (4) sets, compute per-subset pooled $(|\\rho|, d)$, and check whether all seven features receive the same zone assignment in both subsets.\nWe evaluate two complementary splits to avoid dependence on a single partition:\nSplit~A selects \\{Pythia-2.8B, Qwen3-1.7B, OLMo, Mistral\\} (validation: Pythia-1B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B, Llama);\nSplit~B selects \\{Pythia-1B, Qwen3-0.6B, OLMo, Mistral\\} (validation: Pythia-2.8B, Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-8B, Llama).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Holdout split validation of the DDR partition. Selection (sel) and validation (val) $|\\rho|$ and $d$ for two disjoint 4/4 generator splits. Zone assignment is identical across all four subsets for every feature.}\n\\label{tab:holdout-split}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Split A}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Split B}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-5} \\cmidrule(lr){6-8}\n\\textbf{Zone} & \\textbf{Feature} & sel $|\\rho|$ & val $|\\rho|$ & $\\Delta|\\rho|$ & sel $|\\rho|$ & val $|\\rho|$ & $\\Delta|\\rho|$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & $\\bar{H}$ (ent.\\ mean) & 0.60 & 0.58 & 0.02 & 0.60 & 0.58 & 0.02 \\\\\n & TTR & 0.61 & 0.59 & 0.02 & 0.60 & 0.59 & 0.01 \\\\\n & hapax ratio & 0.62 & 0.60 & 0.02 & 0.62 & 0.61 & 0.01 \\\\\n\\midrule\nIntermediate & $\\sigma_H$ (ent.\\ std) & 0.19 & 0.21 & 0.02 & 0.22 & 0.17 & 0.05 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & $H_{\\min}$ (ent.\\ min) & 0.39 & 0.36 & 0.03 & 0.38 & 0.37 & 0.01 \\\\\n & $H_{\\max}$ (ent.\\ max) & 0.05 & 0.05 & 0.00 & 0.04 & 0.06 & 0.02 \\\\\n & tail decay & 0.10 & 0.08 & 0.02 & 0.11 & 0.07 & 0.04 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder simple joint $(|\\rho|, d)$ thresholds, both splits produce a 3/2/2 partition: entropy min is classified as a second intermediate feature due to its elevated pooled $|\\rho|$ (0.36--0.39).\nHowever, entropy min's binary (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) $d$ values are uniformly below 0.06 across all four split${\\times}$subset combinations, consistent with near-stationarity on the task-relevant metric; its pooled $|\\rho|$ reflects change concentrated at the gen-0/gen-1 boundary rather than sustained drift.\nFive of seven features are unambiguously classified under any reasonable criterion; entropy min's boundary status is resolved by the binary-task $d$ criterion.\n\nSelection and validation subsets produce identical partitions in both splits (Table~\\ref{tab:holdout-split}; Figure~\\ref{fig:holdout-scatter}).\nThe maximum $\\Delta|\\rho|$ between selection and validation is 0.05 (entropy std, Split~B), and the minimum gap between adjacent zones remains $>$0.15 in all subsets.\n\n\\begin{figure}[H]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{fig_ddr_holdout_split.pdf}\n\\caption{Holdout split validation in $(|\\rho|, d)$ space. Filled markers: selection set; hollow: validation set. Zone assignments are identical across subsets; selection/validation pairs cluster tightly for all features.}\n\\label{fig:holdout-scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection*{Heteroscedasticity Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:heteroscedasticity}\n\nLevene tests reject equal variance between gen-1 and gen-2+ for drifting features ($p < 10^{-10}$; variance ratios 1.86--2.21).\nTo verify that the DDR partition is robust to this heteroscedasticity, we supplement pooled Cohen's $d$ with Glass's $\\Delta$ (using the gen-1 standard deviation as denominator) and Cliff's $\\delta$ (a non-parametric ordinal effect size; Table~\\ref{tab:effect-sizes}).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Effect size comparison (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, $N$\\,=\\,29{,}095). Glass's $\\Delta$ uses the gen-1 SD as denominator; Cliff's $\\delta$ thresholds follow Romano et al.\\ (2006): $|\\delta| < 0.147$ negligible, $< 0.33$ small, $< 0.474$ medium, otherwise large.}\n\\label{tab:effect-sizes}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Zone} & \\textbf{Feature} & \\textbf{$|d|$} & \\textbf{$|\\Delta|$} & \\textbf{$|\\delta|$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{Drifting} & ent.\\ mean & 1.054 & 1.410 & 0.587 \\\\\n & TTR & 0.814 & 1.116 & 0.510 \\\\\n & hapax ratio & 0.735 & 0.936 & 0.528 \\\\\n\\midrule\nIntermediate & ent.\\ std & 0.457 & 0.605 & 0.295 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{3}{*}{Near-stat.} & tail decay & 0.087 & 0.099 & 0.072 \\\\\n & ent.\\ max & 0.062 & 0.065 & 0.021 \\\\\n & ent.\\ min & 0.016 & 0.015 & 0.354$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\noindent Effect sizes in Table~\\ref{tab:effect-sizes} are computed on the binary task (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+); the main text (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) reports 3-class pooled $d$ values (e.g., hapax ratio $d{=}0.76$), which are slightly larger because the gen-0/gen-1 contrast contributes additional separation.\nGlass's $\\Delta$ amplifies drifting effects (1.41 vs.\\ 1.05 for entropy mean) because gen-2+ variance exceeds gen-1 variance (ratio 2.08), making the pooled Cohen's $d$ conservative.\nNear-stationary features remain negligible under both parametric metrics ($|\\Delta| \\leq 0.099$).\nThe minimum drifting-to-maximum-near-stationary gap is $9.5\\times$ under Glass's $\\Delta$ (0.936\\,/\\,0.099); heteroscedasticity does not erode the partition.\nCliff's $\\delta$ classifies all drifting features as ``large'' ($|\\delta| > 0.474$) and entropy std as ``small'' ($|\\delta|{=}0.295$), consistent with the parametric partition.\n\n\\noindent $^{\\dagger}$Entropy min's elevated Cliff's $\\delta$ (0.354, ``medium'') reflects distributional shape asymmetry between gen-1 and gen-2+ that rank-based statistics detect but location measures do not (Cohen's $d{=}0.016$, Glass's $\\Delta{=}0.015$).\nThis is consistent with its documented boundary status (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}): the gen-0/gen-1 transition produces rank-order shifts that persist into deeper generations without affecting the mean.\n",

    }

1	\subsection{TOST Bootstrap Stability}\label{app:tost-bootstrap}
2	
3	To verify that the equivalence conclusions in \S\ref{app:tost} are not sensitive to sample composition, we assess their stability under bootstrap resampling.
4	For each of four representative features, we run 1{,}000 bootstrap iterations (base seed\,=\,42): each iteration independently resamples with replacement from the gen-1 and gen-2+ pools, recomputes Cohen's $d$ and the TOST $p$-value (equivalence margin $|d| < 0.20$).
5	
6	\begin{table}[ht]
7	\centering
8	\footnotesize
9	\caption{Bootstrap stability of TOST equivalence ($B{=}1{,}000$, margin $|d|{<}0.20$). Rejection rate = fraction of bootstrap iterations where TOST rejects non-equivalence at $\alpha{=}0.05$.}
10	\label{tab:tost-bootstrap}
11	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
12	\begin{tabular}{l c c c}
13	\toprule
14	\textbf{Feature} & \textbf{Rejection rate} & \textbf{Median $d$} & \textbf{$d$ 95\% CI} \\
15	\midrule
16	entropy\_max  & 100\% (1000/1000) & 0.062  & [0.037, 0.088] \\
17	entropy\_min  & 100\% (1000/1000) & $-$0.016 & [$-$0.042, 0.011] \\
18	tail\_decay   & 100\% (1000/1000) & 0.088  & [0.065, 0.111] \\
19	\midrule
20	entropy\_std  & 0\% (0/1000)      & 0.458  & [0.435, 0.479] \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	All three near-stationary features achieve a 100\% rejection rate across 1{,}000 bootstrap iterations, and their effect-size 95\% CIs fall entirely within the $\pm 0.20$ equivalence margin.
26	Entropy std, included as an intermediate control, is never rejected (0\%), with its CI $[0.435, 0.479]$ lying entirely outside the margin.
27	No feature exhibits boundary-crossing: the classification of each feature is unanimous across all bootstrap samples, confirming that the equivalence conclusions are robust to resampling variability.
28

    "newString": "what changes is the \\emph{input}: as the model conditions on increasingly self-typical outputs, expected NLL compresses monotonically (token-level distributions in Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}; per-feature convergence trajectories in Appendix~\\ref{app:convergence}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nRecursive generation alters \\emph{where} the token-level surprise distribution sits but not \\emph{how} it is shaped, a dichotomy with a mechanistic basis.\nUnder nucleus sampling, top-$p$ truncation preserves the functional form of the conditional distribution at each step; what changes is the \\emph{input}: as the model conditions on increasingly self-typical outputs, expected NLL compresses monotonically.\nThis yields two testable predictions: (1)~increasing sampling randomness should weaken the location drift, and (2)~semantic-preservation constraints should attenuate it.\nBoth are confirmed: at $t{=}1.0$, location signal collapses ($\\rho$: $-0.60 \\to -0.002$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); under elaboration prompts, drift weakens (F1\\,=\\,0.730 vs.\\ 0.781; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nCausal analyses provide supporting evidence: drifting features contribute $5.5\\times$ more to classification, with 6/7 exhibiting significant Granger causality over the five-point chain, though the short series length (5 depths) limits formal causal inference (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-causality}).\nThese results are established under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$); shape features remain stable even at $\\tau{=}1.0$ ($d < 0.15$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).\nGreedy decoding ($\\tau{=}0$) confirms shape stability across all eight generators (mean $|d|{=}0.05$), establishing DDR as an autoregressive property independent of sampling truncation; top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$) compresses drifting effect sizes without introducing new shape instability, with boundary violations in Pythia/OLMo traceable to model-intrinsic baselines (Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).\n\nThe classifier is a proof-of-concept for corpus-level auditing, not per-document filtering: gen-1 per-class F1\\,=\\,0.63 is insufficient for individual flagging but supports batch-level contamination estimation (${\\pm}3$--$4$\\% at $n{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}).\nThe three-class resolution enables depth-stratified corpus curation (retaining gen-1 content while flagging recursively degraded material), a distinction invisible to binary classifiers.\nDepth-aware filtering can serve as a prophylactic against model collapse \\cite{drayson_detection_prevents_collapse}, though depth-stratified curation does not yet improve downstream training (TOST equivalence at $\\varepsilon{=}{\\pm}2\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}); the primary value lies in provenance, not training improvement.\n\nDDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse}: retraining erodes tail coverage by updating weights, whereas inference-chain drift at fixed parameters shifts only location, leaving shape stable.\nPrior work establishes \\emph{that} convergence occurs \\cite{markovian_chains,curious_decline} without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved; DDR supplies this decomposition, isolating which distributional properties carry cross-generator depth signal, enabling detection without per-generator calibration.\nThree depth classes define the practical resolution ceiling (5-class F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.50; Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}); QLoRA recovers more signal (F1\\,=\\,0.905, 6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), reflecting token-order patterns that aggregate statistics miss.\nInstruction-tuned models compress DDR structure (3-class F1 degrades 3.7--19.9\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), yet QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91, confirming token-level depth signal beyond summary-statistic decomposition.\nCross-domain evaluation (News, Academic, Code; $t{=}1.0$) yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75 but degraded 3-class F1 (0.62--0.65; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}), with temperature and domain effects confounded.\nOur generation chains use open-ended continuation prompts; real-world recursive pipelines (instruction-following chains, multi-turn dialogues, mixed-model editing) may attenuate, restructure, or introduce confounds not present in controlled chains.\n",

      {

Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}), with temperature and domain effects confounded.\nOur generation chains use open-ended continuation prompts; real-world recursive pipelines (instruction-following chains, multi-turn dialogues, mixed-model editing) may attenuate, restructure, or introduce confounds not present in controlled chains.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nRecursive generation alters \\emph{where} the token-level surprise distribution sits but not \\emph{how} it is shaped, a dichotomy with a mechanistic basis.\nUnder nucleus sampling, top-$p$ truncation preserves the functional form of the conditional distribution at each step; what changes is the \\emph{input}: as the model conditions on increasingly self-typical outputs, expected NLL compresses monotonically (token-level distributions in Appendix~\\ref{app:nll-distribution}; per-feature convergence trajectories in Appendix~\\ref{app:convergence}).\nThis yields two testable predictions: (1)~increasing sampling randomness should weaken the location drift, and (2)~semantic-preservation constraints should attenuate it.\nBoth are confirmed: at $t{=}1.0$, location signal collapses ($\\rho$: $-0.60 \\to -0.002$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}); under elaboration prompts, drift weakens (F1\\,=\\,0.730 vs.\\ 0.781; Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-type}).\nCausal analyses provide supporting evidence: drifting features contribute $5.5\\times$ more to classification, with 6/7 exhibiting significant Granger causality over the five-point chain, though the short series length (5 depths) limits formal causal inference (Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-causality}).\nThese results are established under nucleus sampling ($p{=}0.95$); shape features remain stable even at $\\tau{=}1.0$ ($d < 0.15$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-temp-stability}).\nGreedy decoding ($\\tau{=}0$) confirms shape stability across all eight generators (mean $|d|{=}0.05$), establishing DDR as an autoregressive property independent of sampling truncation; top-$k$ sampling ($k{=}50$) compresses drifting effect sizes without introducing new shape instability, with boundary violations in Pythia/OLMo traceable to model-intrinsic baselines (Appendix~\\ref{app:decoding-strategy}).\n\nThe classifier is a proof-of-concept for corpus-level auditing, not per-document filtering: gen-1 per-class F1\\,=\\,0.63 is insufficient for individual flagging but supports batch-level contamination estimation (${\\pm}3$--$4$\\% at $n{=}1{,}000$; Appendix~\\ref{app:error-analysis}).\nThe three-class resolution enables depth-stratified corpus curation (retaining gen-1 content while flagging recursively degraded material), a distinction invisible to binary classifiers.\nDepth-aware filtering can serve as a prophylactic against model collapse \\cite{drayson_detection_prevents_collapse}, though depth-stratified curation does not yet improve downstream training (TOST equivalence at $\\varepsilon{=}{\\pm}2\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:downstream}); the primary value lies in provenance, not training improvement.\n\nDDR is mechanistically distinct from model collapse \\cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse} (comprehensive supplementary analyses in Appendix~\\ref{app:ddr-supplement}): retraining erodes tail coverage by updating weights, whereas inference-chain drift at fixed parameters shifts only location, leaving shape stable.\nPrior work establishes \\emph{that} convergence occurs \\cite{markovian_chains,curious_decline} without decomposing what drifts from what is preserved; DDR supplies this decomposition, isolating which distributional properties carry cross-generator depth signal, enabling detection without per-generator calibration.\nThree depth classes define the practical resolution ceiling (5-class F1\\,$\\approx$\\,0.50; Appendix~\\ref{app:finer-depth}); QLoRA recovers more signal (F1\\,=\\,0.905, 6-gen LOGO; Appendix~\\ref{app:qlora}), reflecting token-order patterns that aggregate statistics miss.\nInstruction-tuned models compress DDR structure (3-class F1 degrades 3.7--19.9\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:instruct}), yet QLoRA recovers to 0.89--0.91, confirming token-level depth signal beyond summary-statistic decomposition.\nCross-domain evaluation (News, Academic, Code; $t{=}1.0$) yields binary F1\\,=\\,0.74--0.75 but degraded 3-class F1 (0.62--0.65; Appendix~\\ref{app:domain}), with temperature and domain effects confounded.\nOur generation chains use open-ended continuation prompts; real-world recursive pipelines (instruction-following chains, multi-turn dialogues, mixed-model editing) may attenuate, restructure, or introduce confounds not present in controlled chains.\n",
